> @ErstPrinzip _The idea that there was no corruption under the sovjet system is simply incorrect. From the nomenklatura to the apparatchiks, the entire system ran on bribes. Granted it might actually be worse today, though that could just be because there is more available to syphon off._  
> The two-circuit financial system in the USSR contributed to the absence of corruption. It consisted in the fact that there were two currencies inside the country at once: a cash ruble and a non-cash ruble. The population used cash rubles to buy things with them, receive salaries in them, lend to each other, make savings in banks, etc. Non-cash rubles circulated between enterprises. These financial flows did not overlap, they existed in isolation from each other. Because of this isolation, the state could issue non-cash rubles, and this did not accelerate inflation in cash rubles. In addition, having received non-cash money from the state, the plant director had no reason to steal it, since there was nowhere to spend the stolen non-cash rubles, it was impossible to go to the store with them or spend them in any other way. They could only be converted by wire transfer, which was carefully controlled by the state.  
> There were bribes, but the fight against them was relentless. Everyone was held accountable, regardless of their authority, work experience, or characteristics. There is another reason why there was little corruption in the days of Stalin and Brezhnev: the money received could not be legalized.

### **@RomaTomassi** | 👍 59 likes

Hey Jiang — long-time fan of the channel. I’ve nearly finished the Geo-Strategy playlist and have started on the Civilization series. Your work is extraordinary — deeply structural, brilliantly framed, and in many ways savant-level.

One thought keeps tugging at me though — especially given your sympathy for Marx and your repeated critiques of rentier dynamics: Have you ever encountered the Georgist critique? Henry George’s lens on land monopoly and economic rent is like the missing gear in both classical liberalism and Marxism.

Where Marx lumps land and capital together, George separates them — exposing how unearned income from natural monopolies (land, spectrum, network effects) distorts markets, empires, and class structures. In a way, Georgism is a synthesis: it honors production and markets while demanding that the value of location, not labor, be socialized.

You already seem halfway there in your models of empire, scarcity, and elite control. Would love to hear your thoughts if you ever look into it. It might deepen your critique of both the U.S. and China — and recast the coming clash as not just power politics, but a global struggle over who captures the Earth’s unearned bounty.

> ​@asv1452 it sounds like you don't know what to point out. Let's speak briefly about all my claims:  
> 1 - society united around the war - in 2022, 500k-1mil Russians left the country (the exact number is likely between 800k-900k). It is the most significant exodus from Russia since 1917, when the civil war started.  
> While sociology is challenging to perform in a country that punishes opinions different from the official ones, and therefore it might be inaccurate, 2022-2023 reports showed that the Russian society is divided 15-20% pro-war, 15-20% anti-war, and 60-70% without a strong opinion and support official statements. The majority was passive and not willing to sacrifice for anything.  
> A military coup was attempted in June 2023 (Wagner Group rebellion). What is more interesting is that they didn't get resistance from the local population while they were moving to Moscow. If anything, local communities showed support for them. Up to now, Wagner Group patches are common among Russian soldiers, and those are pro-war groups.  
> In summary, the active anti-war minority mostly left the country, and the active pro-war minority dislikes Putin but doesn't see how to replace him. Only the passive majority supports Putin, but it is unclear if another person with a different ideology would replace him.  
> 2 - having respect for soldiers - while official propaganda shows war veterans as heroes, there's not much confirmation that people bought this idea. There are many videos where soldiers who returned from the war describe being rejected by society: they can't find a job, and to get one, they need to lie that they took part in the war, people do not want to rent to them, and so on. The situation is similar to what happened to Soviet soldiers who took part in the Afghanistan war.  
> Also, there have been multiple cases of military cemeteries being set up on garbage dumps, and many more cases being left in disarray. It doesn't look like respect.  
> You can also look at who and get into the army now. What conditions are reported by new soldiers, how wounded are treated, etc.  
> 3 - overall economic success - the message is already too long. Very short - the fast-growing military sector and tremendous amount of money from oil until spring 2023 masked problems in all other sectors. Since the end of 2024, all problems have been visible. They are even reported through Russian state channels.

### **@tokyo169nyc718** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AEPSUC-UQ5k&lc=Ugx1kzspbm-AglIixnN4AaABAg)

35:11
So he says "certain civilizations rebel against slavery, intrinsically" without making it absolutely clear whether he's referring to actual slavery or the abstract concept of slavery through consumerism he's introducing here. But the statement eludes to the notion that other civilizations happily accept slavery. (Again, a fucked up thing to perpetuate whether it's his abstract version of slavery or actual slavery.) 

51:42
He also calls Japan a warrior culture which I was a bit conflicted about. Sure, Japan's samurai/warrior culture has been hyped up throughout modern western media and going back approx. 100 years ago, Japan's own government propagandized samurai-lore to essentially brainwash its citizens to further the nation's imperialistic agenda. But in the modern sense, in 2025, I think to call Japan a warrior culture is really out-dated. 

Current Japanese people in general might be flattered being called a "warrior culture" due to insecurities of lack of actual experience dealing with foreign entities, immature macho BS, current rise of fascism, and the revisionist political propaganda.

However I'm also Japanese, raised mostly by my Japanese grandparents that lived through WWII. My grandfather comes from a samurai class family, my grandmother from a farmer family. Not a single person alive during the war ever spoke of it fondly. That is, if they ever spoke of it. The few times I did hear them speak of it, they cried. My parents' generation mostly never even had the opportunity to hear them speak of it. I've even had the opportunity to visit Hiroshima and listen to a survivor's recounting of it. I take offense to the statement of "Japan enjoys war."

I digress, I was willing to bend somewhat and accept Japan being called a "warrior culture" in contrast to perhaps a society out there in the world that I'm not familiar with that has little to no history of warring. 

But bro hearing Jiang Xueqin say that China isn't a war culture made me eye-roll so hard... Wasn't "The Art of War" written by Sun Tzu? What about the Annexation of Tibet in 1950? Warring States period? Is that too far back? Japanese karate is essentially imported Chinese kung fu... Is that irrelevant to "warrior culture" as well? I don't know my Chinese history enough but something feels off here.

1:03:53 
He explains further when questioned why China isn't a "warrior culture" but IMO it was pretty weak. I found it a bit ironic that the natural defenses/borders argument is also something used by Japan when Japanese explain why we were never colonized by European superpowers or successfully invaded by Mongol/China. And when he mentions Russia's history of being invaded being a traumatic experience for them - thus turning them into a warrior culture, it reminds me of China being defeated by Britain in the Opium Wars and also being invaded by Japan - both which I would also speculate were extremely traumatic experiences for China which by using the same logic as the Professor here I would say accelerated China's authoritarian "warrior culture"... Does that make sense?

I think the framing he uses here is really misleading and it's making me second guess everything he says now. I really enjoyed listening to the consumerism theory and I'm still trying to learn more about Marxism etc. but I gotta say, some of the stuff the professor says is extremely broad and oversimplified but I guess that's what it takes to go viral.

> Sidenote: somewhat silly thing to bring up, but I practiced karate as a child in Japan and was taught not to take my eyes off my opponent when doing the customary bowing before sparring etc to be ready for opponents' low blows or surprise attacks, hell, we were even taught how to stand up properly from sitting position to be able to defend ourselves if someone were to strike us mid-standing up. My point is, fighting dirty is pretty much assumed in actual combat from Miyamoto Musashi's writings even down to some random kid's martial arts school. A code of ethics exist only as a guideline but when it comes to actual life or death there's no holding back blahblahblah. So I need something more than what you provided to consider Sun Tzu to be "anti-warrior" culture.   
>   
>   
> And as for citing Crows Zero (the manga/movie!?) or an anime series about Vikings I'm not familiar with is hilarious. (Don't mean to be rude or anything) I gotta remind you though that stuff is fantasy, although no doubt reflect a culture's values to certain extent, but still, using those to paint a culture's (in this case Japan) in absolutes, as "warrior culture" is extreme.  
>   
>  I'll still counter your point by saying this. I used to love watching One Piece (the anime series) and was moved when Luffy was crying and shouting at his friends to (paraphrasing here) "stop fighting, you guys don't have to win, don't dieee pleaseee, just liiiiiiive! There's no shame in losing, just live!" Which kinda goes against the whole "die with honor" blah blah blah. Y'know I might actually go as far as to say that most Japanese anime stuff is actually anti-war. The whole "die for your country" stuff was really pushed heavy onto the population during WWII by the imperial expansionist government. E.g.,  my grandmother was trained at her high school with a sharpened bamboo stick to stab straw dummies to prepare for when American G.I.'s would eventually invade Japan. My grandmother wasn't good at helping around the farm and was a bookworm and her grandfather paid for her to go to school to pursue her studies, something she was good at, which was rare at the time. She was the only one of her many sisters and brothers to be sent to school. But she was forced to practice stabbing dummies with bamboo spears like that'd actually really do anything against American G.I.s instead of doing what she enjoyed (reading/writing) during the war... I bring that up to say, these type of "die for your country, or else" type of experiences really happened to regular ppl and quite a lot of early anime stuff (movies too) really reflected (IMO) Japanese ppl trying to process those experiences.

I strongly disagree with these objectives. Russia does not pursue any grand global goals; instead, it reacts to the current situation and, more often than not, acts reactively. In other words, it does not make any bold moves but responds to the actions of others, thereby losing the initiative. Society does rally during wartime, that’s true, but it has no effect on fertility: having a child is a threat of ending up in poverty. Corruption hasn’t gone anywhere: it’s present in the army too. The alcoholism factor is downright ridiculous. Who else but the military drinks? 

Russian society reacted poorly to the mobilization. But consumerism won out here too: the army is now being replenished with contract soldiers who receive relatively high pay. Crowds of volunteers didn’t come forward for the sake of a great cause. They came for the money. How could that be?

Putin’s goal isn’t to reshape the nation; his goal is to preserve his country. NATO’s presence in Ukraine is a lever for future pressure. And when Putin dies—or, more likely, steps down from power—and goes to chill in his palace in Gelendzhik, there won’t be a civil war, just as there wasn’t one after Stalin’s death. Everything will play out exactly the same way: a small circle will select and present a figure who suits everyone, and then they’ll cement that choice in the elections.

Alright shit so this one is straight up disturbing. As always his criticisms of the status quo are very astute, "consumerism is the perfection of slavery" is a powerful quote. But I'm VERY put off by his casual glorification of Putin and his obsession with classifying cultures as "warriors" and so on.

When he talks about different countries having different relationships with war due to history -> alright. 

When he says "the Russians are a warrior culture, they are good at war, they ENJOY it", "Russia has been doing BETTER since the invasion of Ukraine", "war is rejuvenating for certain cultures" - brother you sound like a Nazi. This is exactly the sort of social Darwinism, Lebensraum-adjacent 19th century pseudo-sociology that brought us the second world war. It's deeply fucked up. It is deeply, deeply fucked up. 

"War brings people together" no a common enemy does. When Russia invaded Ukraine it exacerbated the latter's national spirit but when Russia invaded Afghanistan, or when America invaded Vietnam, the invader's society was brought to a breaking point. 

"Warriors rebel" no, they don't, like you said an army enjoys having a king. An army functions through authority, hierarchy and discipline. They are not a revolutionary force. If they perceive a leader as weak or meek they might be tempted by a military coup, but this is not the voice of the people. Military coups are almost always followed up by repression, policing and murder of opponents.

>
> @vladislav6800 Uff. I said it's a good start. If you want literature about war econ then check these (have not read but since you're asking for better texts or detailed explanation): "The Economic Effects of War" (Easterlin), "The Macroeconomics of War and Peace"; if you want more Russian-centered literature, then probably these papers/ sources:  
> "The price of war: Macroeconomic effects of the 2022 sanctions on Russia", "What the literature says about the effects of sanctions on Russia", "The Economic Costs of the Russia-Ukraine War: A Synthetic Control Study of (Lost) Entrepreneurship", "DGAP — Russia's war economy",     
> But I explained the main reasoning already: If you produce goods, then yes, it will show in the statistics as higher productivity, people have higher income, etc. BUT the stuff that you are producing is useless - it's produced for the state, often by the state, and just for the war and will be destroyed quite quickly (and, in fact, maintaining it costs a lot of money PLUS conscripted individuals and non-survivors also hurt the econ (because they can't do productive work anymore)).  
> You could also check Russia's national wealth fund (gold and cash reserve it used to have) and how it evolved the past years, or the interest rate. Quote: "As a result, the NWF holds only 153.7 billion yuan (roughly $21 billion) in foreign currency assets, the lowest amount since its creation in 2008. It also has 139.5 metric tons of gold, down sharply from over 400 tons before Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022." Even a short-term lift of (energy-related) sanctions will not reverse this trend and I am not even going into supply chain disruption, cost of infrastructure damage (for Russia: including damaged oil infrastructure, which is notoriously difficult, expensive and lengthy to repair), inflation (inflation because: energy prices go up (yes, counterintuitive first), "everybody" is working for the military (directly or indirectly) hence more people have more money but same amount of goods (remember, military goods are not for the public) = prices go up), lack of meaningful investments (remember the high interest rate?), capital flight, falling imports and exports, etc.  
> There is one caveat though (actually two): If Russia wins the war it can just make the loser pay. This was not part of the initial argument though but I will mention it anyway.  
> Second caveat: If Russia suddenly produced and exported enough HQ military goods to offset the gigantic financial burden the war leads to, then it would work (extremely unlikely though).  
> In summa: In this case producing more does not equal growing wealth. In this case it means a cannibalized economy.  
> This is my general understanding and I am not an economist.

24:12 In the 1980s, money became central to organizing society, marking a "revolt of the elites" against egalitarian trends in the US. This led to the Reagan Revolution and in Britain, the Thatcher Revolution, which introduced a new form of capitalism and an ideology that allowed the elite to amass power. As a result, inequality began to explode.
Society transitioned from being centered around the worker to being centered around the consumer. Governments, which from the 1950s to the 1970s promised good, lifelong jobs, shifted their promise in the 80s to providing low prices and a wide selection of goods. This represented a major political and societal revolution. A worker has a political consciousness to protect their rights through organization, unification, and pushing for favorable political reforms. However, the mentality of a consumer leads to an individualistic, competitive mindset.
Consumerism creates a competition for prestige in society, leading to individualization or the "atomization" of people. This means that individuals are unable to act together. They do not want to organize or act in solidarity.

### **@joeynickles7962**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AEPSUC-UQ5k&lc=UgzNmCwz5lwipv4QpSd4AaABAg)

Hegel’s dialectic is not thesis-antithesis-synthesis.  Hegel’s dialectic is about the internal contradictions within the concept/idea that necessitate the dissolution of that concept in such a way that what is true within is preserved and what is false/no longer useful/archaic is sloughed off.   Synthesis-antithesis-synthesis is Fichte, a later German Idealist.  Also, the full title of the Fukuyama is The End of History and the Last Man.  

Just a couple corrections.  Really enjoying these lectures overall.

### **@edithflood631** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AEPSUC-UQ5k&lc=UgyBAUdDsEgvigw_MlJ4AaABAg)

As  a student of Asian history I must say that the history Professor’s portrayal of China as not a warlike nation is completely wrong. Firstly, the minuscule tiny little but voraciously expansionist Kingdom of Han only became China by subsuming and conquering all the other states around it. These states, many of them possessing Indianized and non-Han cultures, had their cultures appropriated into Han culture, with the Han even inventing legitimizing myths to help falsely assert the cultural artifact was originally Chinese. The best example is the Chu Yuan and dragon boat racing myth, the latter originally being a non-Han tradition. The whole of South China originally being more culturally a part of Southeast Asia falling under the Indian sphere of influence, was gradually incorporated into the Han sphere and this was not a gentle transition. The Miao hill tribes, originally of Mongol stock and pushed into the Southern ranges, put up a hell of a fight against southward Han expansion. The entire story of China’s cultural syncretism (and chauvinism ) is expounded in The Local Cultures of South and East China by Wolfram Eberhard (1969). Then there is the three thousand years of internecine warfare and repeated invasion from the North, (just like Russia), contra what the Professor asserts. The Great Wall was an abject failure and no barrier at all. First the Turks from their then capital in present day Mongolia, the Nuzhen, Mongols, Manchus, finally the Western powers and Japan. China developed military strategy to a superlative degree as a direct necessity. When they were not fighting each other, there were the Mang (Barbarians) to contend with. As a result we are bequeathed numerous deservedly famous tomes of military strategy (Sunzi’s Art of War, and The Thirty-Six Stratagems being the most popular) many interesting histories （Strategies of the Warring States, Records of the Three Kingdoms, and their novel spinoffs)and a few good board games as well (Weiqi, Xiangqi). Of problems facing the Western student of Chinese history is resistance to your penetration of the myth of Han homogeneity. A good start is to look at the origins of the Austronesians, not in Taiwan but right in the cradle of the Yellow River, where the Han also started. Genetic research shows these tattooed riverine and seafaring people, cousins of the Han, moved from the Yellow River into South China as the Bai-Yue, from whence they populated the Pacific. But according to mainland scholars they have nothing at all  to do with China.

### **@jameslooker4791**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AEPSUC-UQ5k&lc=UgxFGn_-23sDvwBNbk54AaABAg)

Based on the Mediazona review of Russian obituaries, the average age of Russian soldiers is in the 36-38 range. Only about 1/4 are under the age of 30. The "old man's war" is not a uniquely Ukrainian problem. It stems from the demographics of both countries and the greater reluctance of young men to volunteer. Middle aged men in Ukraine have the added challenge that they need employment that is seen as valuable to the nation to avoid conscription. The young man working construction might be called an apprentice to avoid conscription, whereas the middle aged guy might be called a laborer. The young man might not have any greater skills than an older equivalent, but he is seen as building skills potentially valuable to the nation in the future versus just a worker.

### **@al_aqua**

24:12 In the 1980s, money became central to organizing society, marking a "revolt of the elites" against egalitarian trends in the US. This led to the Reagan Revolution and in Britain, the Thatcher Revolution, which introduced a new form of capitalism and an ideology that allowed the elite to amass power. As a result, inequality began to explode.
Society transitioned from being centered around the worker to being centered around the consumer. Governments, which from the 1950s to the 1970s promised good, lifelong jobs, shifted their promise in the 80s to providing low prices and a wide selection of goods. This represented a major political and societal revolution. A worker has a political consciousness to protect their rights through organization, unification, and pushing for favorable political reforms. However, the mentality of a consumer leads to an individualistic, competitive mindset.
Consumerism creates a competition for prestige in society, leading to individualization or the "atomization" of people. This means that individuals are unable to act together. They do not want to organize or act in solidarity.

Oh, well, both in the West and in the East, surprisingly little is known about Russia. Of course, one can say that Putin really uses war not only to solve problems related to threats to strategic security, but also to change the ruling elite that does not meet current challenges. And also to restructure the economy, revive industry, and so on. But the point is that humanity is facing a crisis ahead and he is preparing the country for the transition to another era. War for the sake of war does not interest him. Historically, Russia needed access to the seas and control of the straits, and if Russia started any conflicts, it was for these places. Waging war for the sake of territories or resources is the most senseless thing in the case of Russia. The conflict in Ukraine was actually started by the West, and the meaning of the war in Ukraine for Putin is, first of all, the protection of the Russians of Donbass. Who were awaiting the same fate as the Palestinians in the Gaza Strip. Maybe he would not want this, but then he would lose support within Russia and, accordingly, power. This is exactly how the West sees a way out of the current crisis in a war that will destroy China as the main competitor. But without the fall of Russia or its going over to the West, this is extremely problematic. Therefore, when the situation became completely critical, Putin started a preemptive war in order to first bring down Ukraine as a proxy force of the West, and then used it to begin to destroy the West. Putin knows that the agony of capitalism will be bloody and therefore is preparing for the worst-case scenario. Even if you look at the initial events, Putin repeated what happened in Georgia in 2008 - a war for several days, a quick defeat of the enemy, achieving political goals and leaving. No one was going to fight there for several years, or rather, it was a backup scenario in the worst-case circumstances. The West chose a war of attrition and lost. But when clowns and buffoons are at the head of Western countries, what else can you expect? Why doesn't the West and, for the most part, the East know Russia? Because there is no real history of World War II, or rather the part concerning the Eastern Front, which in Russia is called the Great Patriotic War. And this is the key to understanding the actions of both Putin and Russia. Not economics, not strategy, not philosophy - but the history of that war. Almost no one in the West can imagine what it looked like from this side, because the history there was written by former Wehrmacht generals, and NATO was also created by Wehrmacht generals. It was originally an organization with fascist roots. And the Russians know this. Or lose almost 30 million citizens in the most brutal war and you will understand the Russians. It is not for nothing that Putin lays flowers at the Piskarevskoye Cemetery in Leningrad every year. A city where a million people died of hunger and cold. And fascism and the West were to blame for this.

About 10:00 and following, here I have to ask myself the question if it true that the synthesis of capitalism and communism is really liberal democracy. Because while liberal democracy only describes a type of governance, capitalism and communism both describe types of economic systems. And yes I am completely aware that both economic philosophies (especially) communism also have their distinct ideas how a society should be governed, however these economic systems do not exclusively depend on one of the types of governance. For example capitalism is completely fine with a dictatorship monarchy or another undemocratic form of governnance as long as it does not interfere with markets to much.  If you really think about a synthesis of these two economic systems I would say that the more suitable term and system might be the social market economy as found in many European states. There you have Communistic faccettes mixed together with a free market and therefore capitalistic economic system, and yeah these states are also democratic.

### **@AndrewMandrona** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AEPSUC-UQ5k&lc=Ugz31vkHof5tzna-SqV4AaABAg)

The benefits of war come from countries that won soundly or did not fight at all. Russia is essentially fighting on equal footing with poor supply lines, a high casualty rate, and front lines that depend on overwhelming manpower. As a result:

1. The fertility rate in Russia remains low and unchanged. Too many young men are being killed, maimed, or stuck on the front lines.
2. Happiness (although difficult to measure) saw a spike around 2014 after hostilities began, but no improvement when they escalated.
3. Economic troubles are unlikely to go away, as Russia is again driving a weapons-heavy economy. This will help at first but eventually lead to effective inflation for the workers, as it did for the Soviet Union.
4. Alcoholism is actually surging despite the current labor shortage, because the war has exposed millions of people to immense tragedy and uncertainty.


I agree on how Putin was thinking, but it seems like he picked the wrong target. It might have worked if Ukraine fell quickly and Russia entered a new Cold War, but it did not. As for Russia's threat of "Total war", I think the country is realistically there. The threat is only propaganda: They went from "we'll invade!" to "we'll _really_ invade!" to "We can triple production whenever we want, just wait!". Russia lacks the skilled labor and tools to make modern weapons in useful amounts, it's that simple. With a lack of allies, this will continue for Putin's lifetime.

"Religion". Not faith, but the service of the priesthood in Russia has reached such proportions that citizens pay the church for its services.

"Civilization". Russia has numerous internal divisions, and instead of reflecting through empathy, humility, and forgiveness, they have started a war against their brothers.

The problem is that Putin is incapable of empathy and humility. He encourages war and warriors, but with such fundamental attitudes and the problems you mentioned in the lecture, the country's disintegration will only worsen.

The wrong conclusions have been drawn. The root of the problem has not been identified, and the treatment is misdirected.

This is the opinion of a Russian person based on real observations, and not on historical myths, with all due respect to the professor.

However, I get the impression that the Chinese don’t fully understand Russia, Russians, or Putin himself. It’s difficult to translate Confucian patterns of thinking onto the heirs of Byzantine political and cultural traditions and this mismatch could potentially cause problems in the future. ### **@OtsuDC**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AEPSUC-UQ5k&lc=UgwVinkkmX0Au5aYUDV4AaABAg)

1. This is the unconstrained vision Thomas Sowell talks about



2. The pareto distribution is a sort of natural law that arises regardless of the ideological system. Have you ever played monopoly? one person ends up with all the money. If 100 people are in a room each with 1 dollar, and they play a trading game where they flip a coin, and the loser gives up their dollar, one person ends up with all the money. this is a naturally emerging phenomenon that creates inequality, regardless of ideology.



3. You said "when you want to date someone, you don't ask whether they are a nice person or not, you ask how much money they have." Well I have a question. Which sex selects more for money? Women do. Why? Not because of consumerism, but because of natural drives for mate selection that  again, occur regardless of ideology. The point is, hierarchies are naturally occurring, and we cannot treat humanity as if we are blank slates that have no naturally occurring drives. The argument Marx makes, and that you are making here, is a reductive one. there are far more selection criteria for mating than just money, and the sexes value money to different extents regarding mate selection. Women select based on social status. Based on a man's position in the social hierarchy. the form this takes changes, such as a man who rises in the hierarchy through strength and violence in 200 BCE and a man who rises because he is a successful business man in 2025. This inequality was not "manufactured by the elite" it is a natural phenomenon. 



4. Marx posits that there should be a strong central government apparatus that equalizes everything, through the revolution of the proletariat. This doctrine frames the naturally occurring hierarchy as class based discrimination and oppression. Regardless of the inherent problems you run into with that doctrine, the revolution will never end. The reason is because the pareto principle as the mechanism of inequality will never cease to re-emerge as soon as the strong central government starts to "wither away." There will always have to be coercion that takes place in this system. It is a feature of Marxism when taken to it's logical conclusion. Dissent to this revolution is branded as being an "enemy of the people" an "enemy of utopia." Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn documents this with stunning clarity in The Gulag Archipelago. Marxism does not eliminate hierarchy. instead it aligns it with new moral rhetoric speaking of the "bourgeoise" and alignment with this moral rhetoric creates a new selection mechanism that allows people to rise to the top of the hierarchy. The new elite is formed through alignment with the moral rhetoric of the regime. Alignment with the revolution, and against the "enemies of the working class." The hierarchy is ever present. But the selection mechanism takes different forms based on what is valued in society. In the USSR- the intelligentsia, the bureaucrats. Alignment with the rhetoric of equality gives way to the new elite. 



5. hierarchy predates humanity. You can track it throughout the animal kingdom, even to crustaceans who have existed since even before the dinosaurs. The serotonin system that tracks where an organism is in the dominance hierarchy exists even in the simple nervous systems of crustaceans. Hierarchies are even more obvious in lion prides, in primate tribes, etc. For Darwinism to work, there must be a pattern of behavior, a mechanism which an organism embodies in order to move up the dominance hierarchy and achieve unequal mating success. This mechanism, is the same mechanism that nature uses to select. If all organisms were equal, and therefore equal in reproductive success, there would be no evolution or natural selection. Therefore, a hierarchy is a filter, aligning certain embodied or naturally occurring traits with reproductive success. Fitness is always differentiated. 



Marxism in itself, is a rebellion against nature. Inequality is not injustice that is manufactured. It is the injustice implicit in the nature of the world. The question should not be "how do we get rid of hierarchy?" The question should be- "How do we make sure that hierarchy is based on competence, and we all have an equal chance to rise in the hierarchy, given that life itself is unfair?"

Many statements about Russia here were tabloid-grade uninformed.

1. While corruption in Russia is a problem (it is a problem anywhere), Russia is orders of magnitude less corrupt than Europe. When it comes to corruption, EU leads the way, there is no contest. Even Ukraine is not as corrupt as its sovereign.

2. Russia is world's 4th economy. Saying that Russia's GDP is "very low" is just silly. Also, unfiltered GDP including financial bubbles and even debt is a meaningless metric. Russia's GDP also has much larger real sector than any Western country.

3. Alcoholism was an issue 20+ years ago. Half of Russian population today are teetotallers, and most of those who do drink aren't alcoholics. Only 2 million Russians are diagnosed with alcoholism.

4. Putin would not spend two decades avoiding wars to the best of his ability until completely cornered if he actually wanted wars. Putin lost a lot of political capital by not beginning full-scale war against Ukraine back in 2014. Using this war to energize Russia is a real policy, but the war was imposed on Russia externally regardless.

The lecturer is not even familiar with the cultural context, does not know the Russian proverb “a bad peace is better than a good war,” and after the Great Patriotic War, the expression “if only there were no war”(лишь бы не было войны) was widely used by the people like a prayer. The poem by Soviet poet Yevgeny Yevtushenko, “Do Russians Want War?” became his most famous masterpiece and truly part of folklore. It is these things that are still valued today, not military bravado and the desire to destroy everyone who does not think like you.
In 2021, Russia made active attempts and was the initiator of the negotiation process to reduce the intensity of the conflict. Just as in 1914, even after the outbreak of World War I, the Russian Empire did not abandon its attempts at a diplomatic resolution of the situation. This was only ended after Germany declared war on Russia.

This part is a complete failure by the lecturer.
